MetaCheck Report

Video games in cognitive event-related EEG research: A scoping review

2026-11-24 · Metacheck 0.4.4.39

1to fix1to check0to decide by hand1fine12not applicable

To fix (1)

To fix Data Check

What we found

  • We classified 6 files: 5 data, 1 documentation.
  • We found 5 tabular data files and extracted 122 columns from 3 of them.
  • We grouped the files into 1 study group (ex1), plus 1 collection-level file (README/ro-crate metadata).
  • 2 files read as a table but are not a usable rectangular dataset (Fleischmann-et-al_EEG-video-games-scoping-review_data-chart_abbreviations.csv: 100% of columns are free text, not variables; Fleischmann-et-al_EEG-video-games-scoping-review_data-chart_references.csv: 92% of columns are free text, not variables). No columns were extracted and they were not sent to the LLM. Share the underlying data as a plain rectangular table (one header row, one column per variable) with a codebook: Fleischmann-et-al_EEG-video-games-scoping-review_data-chart_abbreviations.csv, Fleischmann-et-al_EEG-video-games-scoping-review_data-chart_references.csv. We ran automated data-quality checks on 122 columns; 32 columns have at least one potential issue (20 columns whose name contains file-illegal characters or is excessively long; 14 columns with leading/trailing whitespace; 2 columns of free text that may contain personal detail; 2 columns with numeric values stored as text). 1 of 1 spreadsheet file has at least one formatting issue (colour coding, merged cells, empty rows/columns, or an offset header).
Show details

This module classifies repository files and, for tabular data files available locally, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

Data Files

Fleischmann-et-al_EEG-video-games-scoping-review_data-chart.xlsx [Data charting form]

Data item Data type Description
STUDY CHARACTERISTICS NA NA
Title Text Full title of the study
Authors Text Full author list
Year Number Publication year
Journal Text Journal name
DOI Text Digital object identifier
Additional study information Text Relevant contextual information not captured elsewhere (e.g., whether the study is part of a larger project, complementary analyses, or reuses an existing dataset)
Main study objective Text Primary research questions and, where explicitly stated, hypotheses
Constructs / Processes / measures of interest Text Key theoretical frameworks, cognitive processes, models, or measures relevant to the study
Rationale for using EEG as measure Text Authors’ reasoning for employing EEG as a measure
Rationale for using VG as paradigm Text Authors’ reasoning for employing a video game as the experimental paradigm
Participants Text Sample size (total and final after exclusions), age, gender distribution, and video game experience where reported; allocation to experimental groups where applicable
Session(s) Structure and Setting Text Timeline and sequence of the session(s), including practice, experimental blocks, and additional tasks; laboratory or field setting characteristics
Experimental Design / Variables Text Groups, conditions, within- and between-subject factors, and dependent measures
VIDEO GAME CHARACTERISTICS NA NA
VG_name Text Name of the video game paradigm; if none is specified, the term(s) used by the authors to refer to it
Source Categorical Origin of the video game. Fixed categories: Commercial; Custom-built
Genre Categorical Video game genre based on visual presentation and core mechanics. Fixed categories: arcade-style; action; simulation; puzzle; sports
Task domains Categorical Predominant behavioral demand(s) on which successful performance depends, independent of genre; one or more may apply. Fixed categories: visuomotor navigation and/or target engagement; first-person shooter; team combat; strategic decision-making; probabilistic (bandit-style) decision-making; Go/No-g…
Concurrent demands (1/2/3+) Categorical Number of independent objectives the player pursues simultaneously, coded at the level of higher-order tasks (e.g., navigating around obstacles while shooting at targets = 2); perceptual, motor, and timing demands nested within a single task are not counted separately. Fixed categories: 1; 2; 3+
Visuomotor control Checkbox Successful performance requires spatially accurate and well-timed movement execution (e.g., steering, dodging, aiming)
Self-paced responses Checkbox Response timing is determined entirely by the player rather than contingent on external cues, time constraints, or spatial constraints
Bounded decision / action epochs Checkbox Gameplay is organized in a trial-like structure of clearly delimited decision or action epochs, as opposed to continuous gameflow
Perspective Categorical Player viewpoint. Fixed categories: interface (abstract visual elements without environmental context); side (lateral or horizontally scrolling view); top-down (bird’s eye view); third-person (viewpoint behind or above the player character); first-person (view through the player character’s visual f…
Visual format Categorical Display dimensionality. Fixed categories: 2D; 3D (movement along three spatial dimensions without immersive technology); VR (immersive presentation via VR headset)
Visual Description / Environment Text Brief description of the visual environment and graphical style
Core Mechanics Text Primary gameplay actions (e.g., navigation, target shooting, response inhibition)
Goal(s) / Instruction Text Task objective and task instructions communicated to the player
VG_player_config Categorical Player configuration. Fixed categories: single; dual; multiplayer
VG_display Text Display device used (e.g., computer monitor, TV screen, VR headset)
VG_interact_device Text Input device(s) used (e.g., keyboard, gamepad, joystick, VR controller)
VG_stimuli Text Relevant in-game stimuli presented to the player
VG_responses Text Player responses (e.g., button press, thumbstick input)
VG_outcome Text In-game outcomes resulting from player actions (e.g., hit, miss, death, score change)
Additional tasks Text Any tasks or conditions implemented alongside gameplay (e.g., secondary oddball task, control conditions, questionnaires administered between blocks)
EVENTS (time-locking points for EEG analyses) NA NA
Events of interest Text In-game events used as time-locking points for EEG analyses.
Comparable events/paradigm in laboratory task Text Laboratory paradigm(s) and event type(s) corresponding to the in-game event(s) of interest (e.g., oddball target, gambling-task feedback)
Event marking method Text Technical method used to synchronize game events with EEG recordings (e.g., parallel port triggers, custom game code, manual frame-by-frame coding)
event_type_stimulus Checkbox Study included task-relevant in-game events that represent stimulus onset events. (One or more event types may apply per study.)
event_type_go-nogo Checkbox Study included task-relevant in-game events that represent go or no-go signal.
event_type_response Checkbox Study included task-relevant in-game events time-locked to player response (e.g. button press).
event_type_error Checkbox Study included task-relevant in-game events time-locked to player responses and analyzed as error-related (e.g., ERN/CRN, Pe).
event_type_feedback Checkbox Study included task-relevant in-game events that represent feedback.
EEG RECORDING & ANALYSIS NA NA
EEG system Text Name and model of the EEG recording system
Scalp location system Text Electrode placement convention used (e.g., international 10-20 system)
Number of electrodes Number Total number of electrodes used for recording
Electrodes Text Specific electrode positions if listed in paper and additional relevant information
EEG sampling rate Number [Hz] EEG sampling rate in Hz
Preprocessing Text Preprocessing methods applied to raw EEG data, including filtering, re-referencing, artifact rejection, and interpolation steps.
EEG Methods Text EEG analysis approaches applied, including event-related analysis methods (e.g., ERP averaging, time-frequency decomposition, spectral power estimation) and any additional analytical steps (e.g., source analysis, composite indices).
Epochs Text Epoch window(s) used for time-locked analyses.
Frequency bands Text Frequency bands defined and analyzed, with corresponding ranges in Hz
eeg_measure_ERP Checkbox Study reported ERPs. (One or more measures may apply per study.)
eeg_measure_TFA Checkbox Study reported time-frequency analyses examining changes in oscillatory power across time and frequency (e.g., ERSP, ERD/ERS, or temporal spectral dynamics evolution of frequency band power).
eeg_measure_ERSP Checkbox Study reported ERSP specifically.
eeg_measure_ERS/ERD Checkbox Study reported ERD/ERS specifically.
eeg_measure_Other Checkbox Study reported other spectral measures including hemispheric asymmetry indices, composite band power ratios, or single-timepoint spectral power estimates.
KEY FINDINGS NA NA
Key event-related EEG findings Text Main event-related EEG findings relevant to the focus of this review, including measure type (e.g., ERP component, frequency band), latency, scalp location, and direction of effect
Interpretation of the authors Text Authors’ interpretation of the reported EEG findings
Methodological / interpretational considerations and potential solutions Text Methodological or interpretational challenges arising from the game-based paradigm and potential ways to address them

To test whether combining EEG signals (with a focus on P300-like responses) from two players in a joint task interacting in a complex environment improves identification of critical, shared in-game events , compared to using either player’s EEG alone.
 P300-like event-related responses; team/joint-task event identification; explicitly differentiated from inter-brain synchrony/hyperscanning approaches, interest here is in exploiting unsynchronized, complementary information from each player’s distinct perspective, not in coupling per se. High temporal resolution; practicality; prior success tracking individual players’ critical events with P300-like responses in single-player VGs (Anderson et al., 2020); potential to inform BCI-applications. Increasingly popular tool to study cognition; complex task that involves perceptual-motor skill learning and allows to study the structure of complex cognition; open-ended task (behavior emerges from interaction between game and player); high rate of player action

Combining EEG signals from the 2 members of a team to improve event identification Fincham JM, Betts S, Anderson JR. 2026 Neuroimage Rep 10.1016/j.ynirp.2026.100356 Two EEG systems (wet/dry) used out of necessity, not choice; cross-system comparison reported in Appendix B. To test whether combining EEG signals (with a focus on P300-like responses) from two players in a joint task interacting in a complex environment improves identification of critical, shared in-game events , compared to using either player’s EEG alone. P300-like event-related responses; team/joint-task event identification; explicitly differentiated from inter-brain synchrony/hyperscanning approaches, interest here is in exploiting unsynchronized, complementary information from each player’s distinct perspective, not in coupling per se. High temporal resolution; practicality; prior success tracking individual players’ critical events with P300-like responses in single-player VGs (Anderson et al., 2020); potential to inform BCI-applications. Increasingly popular tool to study cognition; complex task that involves perceptual-motor skill learning and allows to study the structure of complex cognition; open-ended task (behavior emerges from interaction between game and player); high rate of player action

STUDY CHARACTERISTICS
Title Text Full title of the study
Authors Text Full author list
Year Number Publication year
Journal Text Journal name
DOI Text Digital object identifier
Additional study information Text Relevant contextual information not captured elsewhere (e.g., whether the study is part of a larger project, complementary analyses, or reuses an existing dataset)
Main study objective Text Primary research questions and, where explicitly stated, hypotheses
Constructs / Processes / measures of interest Text Key theoretical frameworks, cognitive processes, models, or measures relevant to the study
Rationale for using EEG as measure Text Authors’ reasoning for employing EEG as a measure

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 122 columns across 4 data files.

Issues Identified

34 file/column combinations have at least one issue.

About the personal-information flags. These are pattern matches, not confirmed disclosures, and they are tuned to over-report rather than miss something. Expect false positives:

  • Column names are matched against identifying words in English and other European languages (email, telefoonnummer, geboortedatum, …). A column called phone_number flags whether or not it holds one, and an empty or already-anonymised column flags on its name alone.
  • Values are matched against the shape of an identifier. An IP address pattern also matches a version string or a dotted numeric code; a card-number pattern requires both a real issuer prefix and a checksum, but a long numeric ID can still coincide.
  • Free text is flagged when a column holds long, mostly-distinct prose — the shape open-ended answers take. Most such columns contain no names at all.

A flag means look at this column. Where the data is already de-identified (an ip column reading Anonymized, a name column the authors emptied), no action is needed.

Spreadsheet formatting such as colour, merged cells, and blank rows/columns is not preserved when data are read programmatically or exported to CSV. Store data as a plain rectangular table (one header row, one column per variable, no colour-encoded meaning) so it is machine-readable.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 4 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

More detail

Ethics Approval Check

The paper does not appear to report data about individual people or animals (we found: a review or meta-analysis named in the title or abstract; a literature search; coding of published papers), so we do not expect an ethics statement.

We based this on:

This scoping review, reported according to PRISMA-ScR, characterizes video game paradigms used in event-related EEG research and relates their findings to canonical laboratory effects.

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.
