More recent work suggests that this apparent negativity may instead reflect a common N2-like response on which a reward-related positivity, the reward positivity (RewP; Holroyd et al., 2008; Foti et al., 2011; Proudfit, 2015), is superimposed. The RewP is elicited by rewarding outcomes and is reduced or absent following non-rewards, giving rise to the apparent FRN difference between outcome types. Whether the FRN and RewP reflect distinct components or alternative descriptions of the same process remains debated, and studies differ in how they quantify and label them. One approach refers to the negative waveform following unfavorable outcomes as the FRN and to the positive waveform following favorable outcomes as the RewP. Another computes difference waves between outcome types, in which subtracting rewards from non-rewards yields a negative deflection (FRN) and the reverse contrast yields a positive deflection (RewP) (Proudfit, 2015; Krigolson, 2018). FRN/RewP amplitude is sensitive to outcome probability and magnitude, with larger responses reported for unexpected or lower-probability outcomes and for outcomes of higher magnitude (Sambrook and Goslin, 2015; Paul et al., 2025). These effects should be interpreted in relation to the task and the way the component is quantified, rather than as invariant properties.

The FRN/RewP is typically followed by a feedback-related P3, more specifically the P3b, which peaks approximately 300-600 ms after feedback onset with a centroparietal scalp distribution (Polich, 2007; Walentowska et al., 2016; Paul et al., 2025). Its amplitude has been linked to outcome evaluation and is often larger for rewards than for non-rewards, for unexpected or lowprobability outcomes, and for outcomes of higher magnitude (Hajcak et al., 2005; Glazer et al., 2018; Stewardson and Sambrook, 2020; Paul et al., 2025).

Relationship between ERN and FRN

The ERN and FRN share a frontocentral scalp distribution, and source-localization and neuroimaging evidence points to a common generator in the medial frontal cortex, most notably the anterior cingulate cortex (Miltner et al., 1997; Holroyd and Coles, 2002; Holroyd et al., 2004; Gehring et al., 2012; Walsh and Anderson, 2012) but they differ in latency and time-locking convention. According to the reinforcement learning theory of Holroyd and Coles (2002), both components reflect a reward prediction error signal, conveyed by the midbrain dopamine system to the anterior cingulate cortex, indicating that an outcome is worse than expected (see also Nieuwenhuis et al., 2004; Walsh and Anderson, 2012).

The two components are thought to differ primarily in the information on which this evaluation is based and, consequently, in the event to which they are time-locked. The ERN (time-locked to action or response) emerges after movement and prior to external feedback and reflects an evaluation based on internal information about the executed response, whereas the FRN (timelocked to external outcome feedback) reflects an evaluation based on external feedback (Holroyd and Coles, 2002; Walsh and Anderson, 2012; Krigolson, 2018). Some studies indicate that their relative contribution depends on how much information about the outcome is available before feedback. The ERN is larger when the response determines the outcome, and the FRN when it does not (Holroyd and Coles, 2002; Walsh and Anderson, 2012). As stimulus-response mappings are learned, the error signal can accordingly shift from the feedback to the response. Frontal midline theta activity, which accompanies both components, has been proposed as a common mid-frontal mechanism signaling the need for control across these and related events (Cavanagh et al., 2012).

Feedback-related time-frequency components Feedback processing is also accompanied by modulations of oscillatory activity (for reviews, see Luft, 2014; Glazer et al., 2018). One commonly reported modulation is an increase in frontal midline theta power (4-8 Hz) approximately 200-500 ms after unfavorable outcomes or negative performance feedback, maximal over Fz and FCz (Cohen et al., 2007; Marco-Pallares et al., 2008; Cavanagh et al., 2010; Van De Vijver et al., 2011). Frontal midline theta is thought to originate in or near the anterior cingulate cortex and to contribute to the FRN. It has been linked to performance monitoring and reinforcement learning, as it scales with reward prediction errors and predicts behavioral adjustment and individual differences in learning (Cavanagh et al., 2010; Van De Vijver et al., 2011; Luft et al., 2013). Its sensitivity, however, is not restricted to negative outcomes. Because frontal midline theta has been found to be also modulated by novelty, conflict, and errors alike, it has been proposed to reflect a domain-general mechanism that signals the need for cognitive control (Cavanagh et al., 2012; Cavanagh and Frank, 2014). Beta activity (13-30 Hz) has been reported to increase after positive feedback and desynchronize after negative feedback over frontal and central sites, within approximately 200-600 ms after feedback onset (Cohen et al., 2007; Marco-Pallares et al., 2008; Van De Vijver et al., 2011; Luft et al., 2013). Two forms of feedback-related beta have been distinguished (Luft, 2014). One is a frontal increase in the beta and low-gamma range after rewards, particularly unexpected ones (Marco-Pallares et al., 2008; HajiHosseini et al., 2012). The other is a sensorimotor beta response to performance feedback in motor tasks, which increases after correct feedback and decreases after errors (Van De Vijver et al., 2011; Luft et al., 2013). Feedback-related beta has accordingly been interpreted both as a reward or learning signal (Marco-Pallarés et al., 2015; Luft, 2014) and as a signal for maintaining or adjusting the current motor state (Engel and Fries, 2010). Owing to its sensorimotor topography, the second form also overlaps with the movement-related beta ERD/ERS described above.

Feedback-locked delta power (below approximately 4 Hz), maximal over parietal sites approximately 100-500 ms after feedback onset, has been reported to be larger after gains than after losses (Bernat et al., 2015; Cavanagh, 2015). Time-frequency decompositions indicate that it contributes predominantly to the feedback-related P3, and in part to the RewP (Bernat et al., 2015).

Event-locked alpha modulations have received comparatively little attention in feedback paradigms. Where reported, alpha decreases are commonly interpreted, as in other contexts, as reflecting cortical activation and attentional engagement (see Alpha and beta ERD/ERS above, Glazer et al., 2018).

Error and feedback processing in the motor domain

The ERN and FRN were originally characterized in tasks in which responses are discrete button presses and errors result from selecting an incorrect response. A smaller but growing number of studies investigates error and feedback processing in more complex motor tasks, such as tracking, aiming, or throwing (e.g., Krigolson and Holroyd, 2006, 2007a,b; Krigolson et al., 2008; Anguera et al., 2009; Vocat et al., 2011; Torrecillos et al., 2014; Maurer et al., 2022). In such tasks, success depends not only on selecting the correct action, but also on how a movement is executed, so that errors can arise during both response selection and response execution (Maurer et al., 2021; Faßbender et al., 2023). Moreover, information about performance accrues continuously during the movement from efferent, proprioceptive, and visual sources, and the terminal outcome may occur only after the movement has ended, as when a thrown ball reaches its target. Internal and external error information can therefore overlap in time, which renders the classical distinction between the ERN and FRN by information source ambiguous (Maurer et al., 2021). Krigolson and Holroyd (2006, 2007a) proposed a hierarchical account of error processing in motor control. This account distinguishes low-level errors, or movement errors, that is, discrepancies between intended and actual movement parameters that can still be corrected during the movement, from high-level errors, that is, failures to achieve the task goal. In a continuous tracking task, highlevel errors elicited a frontocentral ERN-like negativity (Krigolson and Holroyd, 2006), and cues signaling that a tracking error had become unavoidable elicited this negativity before the error itself occurred (Krigolson and Holroyd, 2007b). In contrast, target displacements during aiming movements elicited posterior N1 and P3 components but no ERN, unless corrective movements were blocked so that the target could no longer be reached (Krigolson and Holroyd, 2007a).

Building on this account, Maurer et al. (2021) proposed to distinguish elicited components by the timing of error evaluation relative to terminal outcome feedback. These outcome errors concern failure to achieve the action goal and correspond to the action-effect errors discussed in the main text, as distinct from deviations in movement parameters. In this framework, the ERN reflects the prediction of an outcome error based on any efferent or sensory information available before the outcome is known, whereas the FRN reflects the postdiction of the outcome error once terminal outcome feedback has been presented. Because sensory information about the movement requires additional processing time, ERN-like negativities in such tasks tend to peak later than in speeded choice tasks, for instance, approximately 200-350 ms after ball release in a semi-virtual throwing task (Maurer et al., 2022). In the same task, the ERN increased and the FRN decreased over practice, suggesting that error processing shifts from postdictive to predictive evaluation as an internal model of the task is acquired (Maurer et al., 2022). Meta-analytic evidence further indicates that the FRN is larger in amplitude, shorter in latency, and more variable in latency in motor than in cognitive tasks (Faßbender et al., 2023). The larger amplitude may reflect the higher complexity and feedback ambiguity of motor tasks. The shorter latency may reflect the use of sensory information, such as the visible trajectory of a thrown object, as an early predictor of the final outcome (Faßbender et al., 2023; Maurer et al., 2022). Finally, outcomes in motor tasks are often graded rather than binary, and both ERN and feedback-locked components scale with error or performance magnitude (Anguera et al., 2009; Frömer et al., 2016). In the throwing task, this scaling was present for the FRN from the beginning of practice but emerged for the ERN only after practice (Maurer et al., 2022).

S2. Narrative short summaries

Studies are presented in the order of discussion in the Results section and Tables 1-3 of the main text. Studies that reported EEG activity time-locked to more than one event type are described in each corresponding section, together with the findings relevant to that event type.

Stimulus-related events

(1) Stimulus events conveying goal-relevant information Weidemann et al. (2009) employed a taxi-driving game to investigate event-related spectral changes during object recognition in spatial search. Participants navigated through 3D virtual towns with 6 × 6 grid layouts from a first-person perspective. First, participants had to search for passengers and subsequently deliver them to specific stores. The same stores served either as targets during store search, non-targets (irrelevant stores during store search), or neutral stores during passenger search. The stores' functional categorization was thus based solely on the task context rather than on their sensory features. Because stores emerged continuously as participants moved toward them, precluding precise temporal demarcation of their initial appearance, the authors operationalized stimulus onset as the moment at which stores subtended 0.35% of the display area. At this threshold, target stores were being directly approached in 95% of trials. All store stimuli elicited widespread power decreases in lower frequency bands relative to baseline (delta: 2-4 Hz, theta: 4-8 Hz, and alpha: 8-16 Hz). Target stores were accompanied by a greater frontal theta decrease compared to both neutral and non-target stores, with smaller differences between target and non-target conditions. Notably, all stimulus-condition differences were confined to frontal regions. However, it should be noted that the operational definition of the store stimulus onset likely placed the onset events after the perceptual detection and categorization of the store stimuli by the participants, and perhaps even after the participants' motor response initiation. This potentially complicates the attribution of the observed effects to specific cognitive processes. Chang et al. (2022) focused on alpha-band ERD/ERS (8-13 Hz) in participants interacting with virtual agents in a VR decision-making task, time-locked to when these agents started speaking. The authors compared EEG spectral changes across three forms of multimodal agent communication: embodied (speech and gaze), gestural (speech, gaze, and pointing), and voiceonly (baseline). Participants were seated in a virtual house with bare walls, facing two transparent planes, with the task of ranking 15 desert survival items by dragging them from the left transparent plane to numbered placeholders on the right transparent plane. The participants could make up to 10 help requests, asking the virtual agent for assistance by saying "help". For the embodied and gestural conditions, the virtual agent was visible behind the transparent planes, while in the voice-only condition, the agent was invisible. Alpha ERD/ERS, calculated as the change in spectral power within the 1-second interval after the virtual agent started speaking relative to the 1-second interval before speech onset, varied across the virtual agents' communication forms. Specifically, embodied agents produced alpha power increases in both parietal and occipital regions. Gestural agents produced alpha power decreases in both parietal and occipital regions, while voice-only agents induced alpha power decreases in parietal regions and alpha power increases in occipital regions. The authors related these differential responses to varying speech onset predictability and associated attentional demands across agent communication forms, with embodied agents presenting higher predictability due to the visible virtual body, while voice-only agents presented higher unexpectedness due to the lack of visual cues. Sudden gesturing in the gestural condition required additional attentional resources.

(2) Stimulus events prompting response selection Petras et al. (2016) examined ERPs during a shooting-themed go/no-go task presented in a desert environment with 180° active view control. Participants were instructed to shoot or withhold shooting at human-like avatars based on their appearance as soldiers (go targets) or civilians (no-go targets) under time pressure within a 1-second response window. Each avatar appearance constituted a single decision epoch with immediate visual and auditory feedback following go responses. The study identified several ERP components following avatar onset. An N1 component peaked at approximately 150 ms at Oz, a P2 component at around 200 ms at Fz, an N2 component at around 270 ms at Fz, and a P3 component at approximately 380 ms at Cz. The N2 component showed greater amplitudes for no-go targets compared to go targets, replicating the classical no-go N2 effect (Folstein and Van Petten, 2008), while the P2 and P3 components both exhibited larger amplitudes for go targets. Interestingly, the player's viewing perspective, which varied between an ego and a bird's-eye perspective, modulated several of these components. The first-person perspective produced larger posterior N1 amplitudes in the shooting condition only and larger anterior N2 amplitudes compared to the bird's-eye view. Additionally, the go/no-go amplitude difference in the anterior P2 was more pronounced in the first-person perspective than in the bird's-eye perspective. Sanuki et al. (2022) investigated ERPs and spectral power changes in response to go or no-go stimuli in a custom-built VR game set in a cartoon-like environment. Participants viewed their character from behind on a path bordered by trees, with stones serving as obstacles and circles serving as targets within the path. In addition, monsters with jewels appeared in front of the video game character. With the video game character continuously moving forward, participants had to navigate laterally to collect targets and avoid obstacles while simultaneously responding to color changes of the monster's jewel and the character's clothing. Responses were required when colors differed (go), and response inhibition was required when colors matched (no-go). The study further differentiated between successful and failed responses. ERP analyses time-locked to the color changes revealed that both successful and failed no-go trials, as well as successful go trials, elicited N2 and P3 components at Cz. In contrast, failed go trials were characterized by an attenuated N2 and a complete absence of the P3 component. In addition, frontal theta power preceding stimulus onset was higher in successful than in failed go trials. Qiao and Iramina (2026) extended the paradigm used by Sanuki et al. (2022) into a 10session training study, using the same custom game platform. EEG was recorded during the preand post-test multitask blocks, with analyses time-locked to the color-change onset of the colorchange go/no-go task. Participants were assigned to one of three gaze-strategy groups based on fixation-pattern clustering across the 10 training days: target-focused, distributed, and color-cuefocused. Post-versus pre-training changes differed by gaze-strategy group. The target-focused and color-cue-focused groups both showed a larger no-go N2 (200-400 ms, Cz) after training, though the increase was markedly larger in the target-focused group than in the color-cue-focused group. The target-focused group additionally showed a larger no-go P3 (400-600 ms, Cz and Fz), while the color-cue-focused group instead showed a smaller go P3 (400-600 ms, Fz). Both groups also showed increased midline theta power (200-600 ms, Fz), with the color-cue-focused group additionally showing increased alpha power (Fz, Cz, Pz) and increased frontoparietal theta connectivity. The distributed group showed little change in any measure. Across participants, larger post-training increases in parietal no-go P3 (400-600 ms, Pz) and frontal theta power (200-600 ms, Fz) were associated with greater gains in performance on the color-discrimination task (CDT) during multitasking. Maclin et al. (2011) analyzed EEG time-courses following video game events in Space Fortress, a space-themed game custom-built for research that integrates multiple cognitive tasks (Donchin, 1989). Participants view a space battle in a hexagonal arena with plain graphics in a 2D display. Their task is to navigate a spaceship by applying thrusts in a frictionless space, destroying a hostile fortress in the center, avoiding damage, and identifying appearing mines as friends or foes. Mine appearances elicited a P3 component peaking around 500 ms at frontal and parietal sites, which tended to be larger at frontal sites. Mine onsets were additionally followed by a transient alpha (10-15 Hz) power increase between 300 and 700 ms, which was stronger after training. Unlike the P3 to fortress hits in the same study (see Feedback-related events below), the mine-onset P3 did not decrease with training and tended to increase. The authors attributed this to the changing letter mappings across blocks, which prevented automatization of mine identification. Participants additionally performed an auditory oddball task, both alone and concurrently with gameplay (see Feedback-related events below).

(3) Stimulus events signaling changes in the conditions of ongoing play Spüler and Niethammer (2015) employed a custom-built arcade-style game with simple abstract graphics in which participants controlled the cursor movement angle with a gamepad thumbstick to avoid collisions with blocks falling at a constant speed from the top of the screen. The cursor was controlled by the participants continuously throughout the task. The researchers examined neural responses to abrupt, system-initiated perturbations in cursor control, during which the cursor's movement direction was offset from the participant's thumbstick input by 45°, 90°, or 180° to the left or right for a duration of 2 s, thereby transiently modifying the response-outcome mapping. These perturbations occurred at randomized intervals of 5 to 8 seconds. EEG analysis revealed neural responses time-locked to perturbation onset that were maximal at frontocentral electrodes (FCz and Cz), with a positive peak at 229 ms, a negative peak at 287 ms, a positive peak at 367 ms, and a small negative peak at 461 ms. Wimmer et al. (2024) investigated neural responses to system perturbations in a VR gamelike flight simulation with continuous control, in which participants controlled a virtual glider moving at a constant speed by steering left, right, up, or down through ring-shaped targets. Perturbations were introduced in the form of either sudden target jumps, for which the target was relocated to the left or right, or glider rotations, for which the glider was rotated horizontally, thereby disrupting the participant's intended flight path. EEG analysis focused on low-frequency activity (1-10 Hz). Neural responses at frontocentral sites showed a similar sequence following both perturbation types, with slightly delayed latencies for glider rotations. A positive peak at 188 ms was followed by negative deflections (234 ms for target jumps, 250-266 ms for glider rotations), positive deflections (313 ms, 344-391 ms), and negative deflections (469 ms, 484-500 ms). Center et al. (2026) combined an immersive VR task with externally imposed, systemgenerated interruptions of gameplay. Participants played a VR slingshot arcade game, launching a ball at color-coded targets viewed in first-person via a head-mounted display. The main objective was to investigate neural signatures of presence, which was manipulated at the block level through embodiment (tracked first-person avatar vs. a stationary avatar controlled via handheld thumbsticks). Because the embodied condition additionally introduced natural hand-tracking and controller vibration feedback -factors we treat as exclusion criteria elsewhere in this review -we focus here on the non-embodied (standard-controller) condition. The main events of interest were breaks in presence (BIPs): on 20% of trials, the display froze for 1000 ms immediately after release, temporarily disrupting the game's sensorimotor flow. ERPs were time-locked to the release of the ball from the sling, which by design coincided with BIP onset. Because the BIP manipulation was both an externally imposed disruption and time-locked to participants' own self-initiated response (the release of the ball from the sling), we report the later components here and the readiness-potential findings in the response-related section. BIP trials elicited a more negative central N400 (380-580 ms) than normal trials, and a central P3b (300-380 ms) that was reduced for BIP relative to normal trials. The N2 did not differ between freeze and normal trials. Bernal et al. (2024) employed the game Tetris in a virtual reality (VR) environment to investigate spectral modulations associated with what the authors define as a "helper-piece" event, namely the appearance and descent of a special ball-shaped piece that, once placed, clears four rows and consequently reduces the overall difficulty of the game. The helper-piece was triggered once the Tetris stack attained 60% of the playing field height, while the participants only knew that the helper-piece would appear at an unspecified time during gameplay. The occurrence frequency of the helper-piece was not reported. Spectral analysis revealed broadband power increases before the clearing, with frontal alpha (8-13 Hz) power rising approximately 4 s and frontal and parietal beta (15-30 Hz) power rising about 3 s before the event. Following the helper-piece event, parietal alpha power decreased from 0 to 1 s, while parietal beta power increased between 1 and 3 s. A frontal theta power decrease was also observed 2 to 4 s following the event. The authors associated the pre-event spectral changes with increasing task difficulty before the helper-piece event, and the post-event spectral changes with game state reassessment following the game's difficulty reduction.

Response-related events

(1) Preparation of self-paced actions Verbaarschot et al. (2019) examined the neural correlates of button presses in the custombuilt strategic decision-making game Free Wally, comparing them to those elicited by a control task that employed visually similar but abstract stimuli devoid of narrative context. In the Free Wally game, participants were required to make strategic decisions aimed at rescuing a whale by directing water jets either to the left or right in order to displace humanoid agents from a hill while simultaneously managing limited resources and game-level temporal constraints. Players chose the timing of individual button presses within these broader game-level constraints. Each decision epoch corresponded to one virtual day and concluded with an outcome presentation, thereby imposing a discrete trial structure onto the overarching game narrative. The control condition featured spontaneous button presses without meaningful goals or consequences. Although the principal objective of the study was to compare neural preparatory activity underlying deliberate versus spontaneous actions, the present review primarily focuses on how the incorporation of narrative context and a game-like framework influenced response-related EEG signatures. EEG analyses time-locked to self-paced button presses revealed typical movement preparation patterns for both tasks: RP as a slow buildup of negative potential maximal at Cz beginning 2000 ms before responses (Libet et al., 1983; Shibasaki and Hallett, 2006), an LRP emerging approximately 200-400 ms before the button press at C3/C4 (Trevena and Miller, 2002; Smulders and Miller, 2012), and central alpha/beta (8-30 Hz) ERD from 2 s before action onset, followed by ERS at 500 ms post-action (Pfurtscheller and Berghold, 1989; Pfurtscheller and Lopes da Silva, 2017). Crucially, the comparison of RP, LRP, and ERD between the two task conditions revealed no significant differences. The comparison therefore did not detect differences in the measured movement-preparation signatures attributable to narrative context and meaning. Center et al. (2026) examined EEG activity time-locked to participants' self-paced release of a ball from a slingshot in an immersive VR game, in which release could trigger an externally induced display freeze. Beyond the stimulus-related P3b and N400 effects reported in the stimulus-related section, the authors also examined response-locked activity preceding this release. Although not a planned component of interest, the authors reported a slow negative buildup beginning approximately 400 ms before ball release and maximal over central electrode sites, consistent with an RP.

(2) Action selection and execution during ongoing visuomotor control Mariman et al. (2023) used a custom-built 2D visuomotor game with abstract graphics that required participants to navigate a circular cursor through obstacles while simultaneously shooting at targets. Gamepad controls were perturbed with a 90° rotation for movement and elastic resistance for shooting, introducing unintuitive controls to which participants had to adapt. Participants trained the two control components either together (whole practice) or separately (part practice). Performance improved with training as participants adapted to the modified motor requirements. Response onsets were defined as the displacement of the left or right gamepad thumbstick for navigation or shooting responses, respectively. In their analyses, the authors included only events without temporal overlap with other events occurring within 500 ms before and 300 ms after event onset. Spectral analyses revealed beta-band ERD (12-30 Hz) around the onset of navigation responses and ERS following shooting responses, most clearly in the part-practice group. However, because the study primarily investigated training-induced modifications, the exact temporal characteristics and spatial distribution of these fundamental patterns were not delineated. Practice-related ERD/ERS changes may also partly reflect changes in thumbstickdisplacement duration, as navigation movements were shorter on the first day than in the final session. Anderson et al. (2020) analyzed ERPs time-locked to participants' responses in Autoturn, a simplified variant of the Space Fortress game (see Maclin et al., 2011 above). In this variant, the spaceship automatically targets the fortress in the center. As in the standard variant of Space Fortress, participants were required to navigate the spaceship between two hexagons by applying thrust, destroying the fortress, and avoiding the spaceship's destruction from fortress shells or hexagon collisions. Although players decided when to press, thrust responses were largely shaped by the spatial demands of navigation, such as avoiding the hexagon borders, and fire responses carried an additional pacing demand: Fortress destruction required participants to build fortress vulnerability through 11 shots spaced at a minimum of 250 ms intervals, followed by one further shot within 250 ms. Importantly, participants used the left hand for pressing the thrust button and the right hand for pressing the fire button. Consistent with the contralateral localization of the LRP (Trevena and Miller, 2002; Smulders and Miller, 2012), the study found greater rightlateralized central negativity preceding thrust button presses and slightly greater left-lateralized frontocentral negativity preceding fire button presses. Cavanagh and Castellanos (2016) examined response-locked ERPs in a custom arcade-style video game called Escape from Asteroid Axon. In this game, players navigate a starfighter through a continuous environment, collect stars and ammunition boxes while avoiding collisions with enemies or walls, and fire missiles at enemies. Of the six distinct video game events examined by the authors, one was response-related (missile launches), while the remaining five were feedback-related and will be discussed below. Missile launch button presses elicited a frontocentral negativity in the delta band, with an exact range not specified, that peaked around the button press. The authors attributed this effect to action selection processes previously reported by Cavanagh et al. (2012).

(3) Response-related performance monitoring Mariman et al. (2023) examined response-locked performance-monitoring activity in a custom-built 2D visuomotor game combining navigation and shooting. Participants shot at targets by displacing the right gamepad thumbstick against additional elastic resistance and trained navigation and shooting either together (whole practice) or separately (part practice). Timedomain analyses of shooting epochs showed positive ERP deflections peaking approximately 200 ms after movement end (600 ms after displacement onset), which the authors interpreted as error-related positivity (Pe). Following training, these deflections showed decreased amplitudes in the whole-practice group, paralleling the reduction in errors. Arake et al. (2022) used a first-person military shooting game in a VR environment to measure ERPs time-locked to gunshot responses. Participants navigated through devastated outdoor environments and warehouses to reach goal points while encountering armed enemies that they were required to shoot by pressing the gamepad's "R" button. Following each shot, a 500 ms recharge time was imposed before the next shot could be fired. The authors' EEG analyses were restricted to shots that were separated by a minimum of 800 ms from both the preceding and subsequent shots in order to prevent temporal overlap between epochs. At frontocentral electrodes, negative deflections peaking at around 100 ms after the response were detected, with larger amplitudes for missed shots than for hit shots at electrode FFCz. The authors classified these components as ERN for missed responses and CRN for hit responses based on their temporal, spatial, and morphological characteristics. ERN amplitudes recorded at the more posterior CCPz electrode demonstrated positive between-subjects correlations with behavioral performance measures. Specifically, larger (i.e., more negative) ERN amplitudes were associated with a reduced number of shots required per kill and a shorter time required per kill. Notably, misses were approximately four times more common than hits. The authors attributed this to the difficulty of the required motor response, which involved both joystick-based aiming and precise trigger timing. The dominant ERN source was localized to the rostral rather than the dorsal anterior cingulate cortex, which the authors related to negative-affect processing associated with the threat of being hit.

Feedback-related events

(1) Feedback-related P3 Anderson et al. (2020) used the Autoturn variant of Space Fortress to investigate EEG responses to rewarding fortress destruction (kill ) and two punishing events. These were the destruction of the participant's spaceship (death) and the restoration of fortress invulnerability when players fired too quickly (reset). Kill events elicited a general negativity at 100 ms and an anterior positivity at 300 ms post-event. Death events elicited a strong central positivity, peaking approximately 400 ms after the event. Reset events resulted in a strong central negativity at 300 ms post-event, followed by a stronger central positivity at 500 ms. The authors classified the post-event positive deflections associated with kill, death, and reset events as instances of the P3 response. The amplitude of these P3 responses systematically varied as a function of event probability, such that the most frequent events (kills) elicited the smallest P3 amplitudes, whereas the least frequent events (deaths) elicited the largest amplitudes. P3 latency also differed between events, peaking earliest after kills (approximately 300 ms) and latest after resets (approximately 500 ms). The authors attributed this to differences in predictability: kills and deaths could be partly anticipated from the preceding build-up of fortress vulnerability and the ship's flight dynamics, whereas resets occurred abruptly whenever players fired too quickly. Anderson et al. (2024) employed a co-op variant of Space Fortress, retaining the abstract graphics and frictionless flight mechanics while requiring coordination with an automated bot. Unlike in the standard variant of Space Fortress, fortress vulnerability did not need to be built up. Instead, the bot acted as bait, prompting the fortress to fire and expose its rear shield, allowing the participant to maneuver behind it and destroy it. The authors computed ERPs for three critical events representing rewarding or punishing outcomes of varying magnitudes: fortress destructions (kill event, +100 points), ship destructions (death event, -100 points), and missed shots (miss event, -10 points). All event types elicited positive deflections, which the authors classified as P3 variants, and the amplitudes varied by outcome magnitude, being largest for deaths and weakest for misses. In additional analyses, the researchers applied principal component analysis to isolate ERP components that discriminated between the different event types. One resulting principal component separated punishing outcomes (deaths, misses) from rewarding outcomes (kills), with a stronger association for more severe penalties (deaths vs. misses). This principal component exhibited a small negative deflection at approximately 250 ms at electrodes Fz and Cz, which the authors suggested may correspond to the ERN/FRN complex. This was followed by a pronounced positive deflection, which the authors attributed to the P3. Fincham et al. (2026) used the co-op variant of Space Fortress with both roles filled by human players, examining whether combining EEG signals from both members of a two-player team improves identification of the same critical in-game events studied by Anderson et al. (2024) fortress destructions (kill events) and player deaths -now differentiated by which team member, the Bait or the Shooter, was destroyed. All three event types elicited positive deflections at central electrodes (Fz, Cz, Pz) that the authors again classified as P3-like responses. Further, they reported a role-by-event interaction: Shooters showed larger and earlier responses than Baits following kills and Shooter deaths, whereas Baits showed larger, though later, responses following their own deaths. Regardless of role, each player produced their largest and fastest response to their own death rather than to the fortress kill or to the other player's death. This pattern was mirrored in the classification results, where combining both players' EEG best identified kills (i.e., events witnessed by both players) while identification of deaths relied more on the response of the player who had actually died. Yokota et al. (2019) investigated brain activity during a competitive baseball video game in which two players competed either as a batter or a pitcher. The authors examined ERPs that were time-locked to two types of pitches: swinging strikes, when the batter swung and missed, and called strikes, when the batter held back but the pitch still passed through the strike zone. Events were identified via a visual analysis of recorded game footage, marking frames where the event outcomes became definitively apparent. Both strike types elicited a frontocentral positive potential between 250 and 350 ms post-event onset in both batters and pitchers, which the authors identified as P3. Swinging strikes also elicited a frontocentral negative potential between 200 ms and 0 ms pre-event onset in batters, i.e., the error-committing player only, but not in pitchers. Since both players viewed the identical visual scenes, the authors attributed this batterspecific response to subjective error experience rather than visual differences, identifying it as ERN. The amplitude of this negativity also scaled with the score difference, being larger when the batter was losing. Called strikes also showed negative potentials in batters, though with smaller amplitudes. Li et al. (2025) recorded EEG during gameplay of Honor of Kings, a mobile multiplayer online battle arena (MOBA) game in which two five-player teams compete to destroy the opposing base. This study did not test a specific hypothesis. Instead, the authors provided an open EEG dataset spanning six matches per participant and reported event-related findings for critical events (kills and deaths) as part of its technical validation, alongside resting-state EEG and gaming-addiction-related psychometrics. Kills and deaths were identified via frame-by-frame video coding of scoreboard increments by two independent reviewers, with event timestamps corrected for a measured screen-mirroring delay and aligned to the EEG signal via a calculated recording-onset offset. Both kills and deaths elicited a frontocentral P3 at FCz (250-450 ms), accompanied by delta-band power increases (kills: 250-450 ms at Fz; deaths: 250-450 ms at FCz) and theta-band power decreases (kills: 750-1200 ms at Fz; deaths: 550-1800 ms at FCz). Kills additionally elicited a parieto-occipital theta increase (POz, 300-600 ms), while deaths elicited a centroparietal beta increase (CPz, 400-1000 ms). Maclin et al. (2011), discussed above for its results on stimulus-related ERPs and ERSPs using the Space Fortress video game, also reported EEG responses to feedback events. Specifically, the authors identified a P3 component peaking around 400 ms in response to successful fortress hits, accompanied by event-related delta (1.6-5 Hz) power increases around 500 ms after fortress hits at electrodes Fz and Pz. In addition to administering the Space Fortress paradigm in isolation, the authors also acquired EEG data while participants performed an auditory oddball task both as a standalone condition and concurrently with Space Fortress gameplay. This design enabled a systematic examination of the distribution and allocation of attentional resources under competing task demands. Training on the Space Fortress game produced divergent P3 amplitude changes -reduced for fortress hits and increased for oddball tones -replicating the canonical resource reciprocity pattern from laboratory dual-task paradigms (Polich, 2007). Deltaband ERSPs showed the same cross-over pattern, decreasing for fortress hits and increasing for oddball tones with training, and the training-related changes in delta power for fortress hits were negatively correlated with those for oddball tones. A corresponding correlation for P3 amplitude was not significant. The authors attributed this pattern to the liberation of attentional resources as the primary task became automatized through training, thereby permitting the reallocation of cognitive capacity to the processing of the secondary task. Pei et al. (2025) recorded EEG while participants played Flappy Bird, a side-scrolling arcade game rebuilt for this study, in which participants navigated a bird through a series of vertical pipes by tapping the screen to keep it airborne, and compared the resulting event-related responses to those elicited across five conventional laboratory tasks (visual oddball, auditory oddball, semantic violation, emotion matching, and reinforcement learning) completed by a separate cohort under equivalent settings. Pipe-hit events, marking in-game punishment, elicited a large centroparietal positive deflection emerging at approximately 180 ms and peaking around 300 ms at Cz and Pz, which the authors termed P300-CE and which exceeded the ERPs from all five laboratory tasks in envelope amplitude, within-and cross-participant reliability, and signal-to-noise ratio. While the ERP amplitude declined across repeated trials in all five laboratory tasks, the pipe-hit component showed no such decline, instead remaining stable or even increasing in amplitude over successive blocks. A markedly smaller positivity followed pipe passes. The P300-CE component with similar non-adapting and large amplitude characteristics was subsequently replicated in an additional sample of 10 participants using a minimalist custom-built game (Space Escape) and a physical toy task (Crocodile Dentist), following collision and snapping events, respectively. Cavanagh and Castellanos (2016) examined five feedback-related events in the authors' custom-built space shooter video game, comprising three rewarding events (star collection, ammunition collection, and missile hits on enemies) and two punishing events (wall crashes and enemy crashes). One of the rewarding events, missile hit, elicited ERPs with a peak around the P2 latency (approximately 200 ms post-event) at electrode Cz. The authors tentatively associated the plateau following the P2 with the RewP and noted accompanying increased delta-band power. In contrast, the remaining reward-related events (i.e., star collection and ammunition collection) elicited only weak neural responses that did not differ reliably from those observed during null trials. The punishing events, wall/enemy crashes, also elicited a pronounced positive deflection at Cz approximately 200 ms following event onset, accompanied by increased delta/theta-band power. This did not amount to the pronounced frontal midline theta enhancement observed for gambling losses in the same participants. However, the authors did not formally characterize this activity in terms of specific component polarity or latency parameters. To further elucidate the psychological significance of the video game-related events, the authors additionally implemented a cross-paradigm validation strategy. Specifically, they employed pattern classification methods to compute classifier weight vectors from EEG data acquired while the same participants performed canonical cognitive tasks (oddball and gambling paradigms), and subsequently applied these weights to the video game events using a transfer learning framework. All major goal-oriented video game events demonstrated significant transfer bias toward oddball target detection in the canonical P3 time range and toward gambling wins for hits. Punishing crashes showed no comparable transfer toward gambling-loss weights.

(2) Feedback-and error-related potentials Yokota and Naruse (2021) examined ERPs time-locked to hits and misses in a custom-built VR shooting game, specifically targeting the FRN (termed FN in the original study) and RewP. Participants used a handgun to shoot down incoming cannonballs from a first-person perspective. Hits caused the cannonball to disappear, while misses resulted in the player being struck and losing a life point. The EEG results were presented in the form of both condition-specific waveforms and corresponding difference waveforms. Negative potentials followed miss events, and positive potentials followed hit events, both emerging approximately 200-250 ms after feedback at electrode FCz, and were identified as the FRN and RewP, respectively. For the miss condition, the FRN was followed by a subsequent positive deflection around 250-400 ms. For the hit condition, the positive deflection exhibited a bimodal peak, suggesting that the RewP might be superimposed on a later P3 component. The difference waveform computed as miss minus hit exhibited an early negative deflection with a peak latency of approximately 200 ms, followed by a subsequent positive deflection occurring within the 300-500 ms post-stimulus interval. The FRN amplitude was larger in easier, high-probability situations (operationalized via task difficulty parameters, i.e., cannonball size and trajectory curvature), in which misses were unexpected, and during periods of better recent performance ("positive mood"). RewP amplitude was larger in harder, low-probability situations and under higher outcome magnitude, operationalized as performance pressure derived from the remaining time and accumulated damage count. Anderson et al. (2024) examined feedback-related potentials in the co-op variant of Space Fortress, in which a participant cooperated with an automated bot to destroy a fortress. Principal component analysis isolated a component separating punishing outcomes (deaths, misses) from rewarding outcomes (kills), with a stronger association for more severe penalties (deaths vs. misses). This component exhibited a small negative deflection at approximately 250 ms at Fz and Cz, which the authors suggested may correspond to the ERN/FRN complex, followed by a pronounced positivity attributed to the P3. Cavanagh and Castellanos (2016) examined feedback-related events in the custom-built space shooter Escape from Asteroid Axon, in which players collected stars and ammunition, fired missiles at enemies, and avoided collisions. Missile hits elicited a P2-like peak approximately 200 ms after the event at Cz. The authors tentatively associated the subsequent plateau with the RewP. Star and ammunition collections elicited only weak responses that did not differ reliably from null trials, whereas wall and enemy crashes elicited a pronounced positive deflection approximately 200 ms after the event at Cz. Martínez-Tejada et al. (2021) employed a custom-built, space-themed video game in which participants controlled a horizontally moving spaceship that could be moved vertically to collect rewarding tokens (e.g., astronauts, coins) while avoiding punishing tokens (e.g., asteroids, enemies). The collection of rewarding tokens elicited potentials peaking around 200 ms following event onset, resembling the P2. Punishing events produced immediate ERP peaks at collision onset that were preceded by a negative amplitude beginning approximately 500 ms before event occurrence. The authors suggested that this pre-event activity might reflect participants' anticipation of impending negative events. Although the authors reported that these patterns were observed across multiple electrodes, they did not provide a precise characterization of the corresponding scalp topography, beyond presenting data from the FCz electrode as a representative example. Anderson et al. (2020) examined EEG responses to fortress kills, ship deaths, and vulnerability resets in the Autoturn variant of Space Fortress, in which players navigated a spaceship and fired at a fortress. In addition to the P3 responses described above, kills elicited a general negativity at 100 ms, and resets elicited a central negativity at 300 ms. The authors did not assign these negativities to specific components. Spüler and Niethammer (2015) examined ERPs elicited by collisions with falling blocks in a continuous-control game in which participants steered a cursor to avoid them. Collision-related ERPs demonstrated maximal activity at the FCz and Cz electrodes, with an early negative peak at 2 ms, which the authors identified as ERN, followed by a positive peak at 268 ms, which they identified as Pe, a negative peak at 486 ms, and a small positive peak at 742 ms. The authors attributed the first two peaks to error processing, suggesting that participants anticipated collisions before they occurred. Yokota et al. (2019) investigated EEG activity in a competitive baseball video game in which two players acted as batter and pitcher while viewing the same screen. Events were marked from recorded game footage at the frame where the outcome became definitively apparent. Swinging strikes elicited a frontocentral negativity between 200 ms and 0 ms before outcome onset in the batter, the error-committing player, but not in the pitcher. The authors interpreted this batterspecific response as an ERN reflecting subjective error experience rather than visual differences. Its amplitude was larger when the batter was losing. Called strikes also elicited negativities in batters, but with smaller amplitudes. Both strike types were followed by a frontocentral positivity between 250 and 350 ms in both players, identified as P3.

(3) Spectral modulations associated with game outcomes Cavanagh and Castellanos (2016) examined spectral activity associated with rewarding and punishing events in Escape from Asteroid Axon, a space shooter in which players collected stars and ammunition, fired missiles at enemies, and avoided collisions. Missile hits elicited increased delta-band power, while wall and enemy crashes elicited increased delta/theta power. However, crashes did not elicit the pronounced frontal midline theta enhancement associated with gambling losses in the laboratory task completed by the same participants. Star and ammunition collections elicited only weak responses that did not reliably differ from null trials. Li et al. (2025) recorded EEG during Honor of Kings, a mobile multiplayer online battle arena game in which two five-player teams compete to destroy the opposing base. Kills and deaths elicited delta-band power increases (kills: 250-450 ms at Fz, deaths: 250-450 ms at FCz), alongside the P3 responses described above. Both events also elicited theta-band power decreases (kills: 750-1200 ms at Fz, deaths: 550-1800 ms at FCz). Kills additionally elicited a parietooccipital theta increase (POz, 300-600 ms), while deaths elicited a centroparietal beta increase (CPz, 400-1000 ms). Salminen and Ravaja (2007) employed the commercial video game Super Monkey Ball 2. In this game, participants tilt a game board hanging in mid-air to steer a monkey encased in a transparent ball toward a goal while collecting tokens (bananas) for bonus points and avoiding falls from the board's edges. The study analyzed spectral changes evoked by three game events, comparing activity 1 second before versus 1 second after event onset: collecting one or more tokens, falling off board edges, and reaching the goal. The authors characterized token collection and goal reaching as rewarding events while noting that the fall event's valence was ambiguous. As tokens were often positioned near hazardous board edges, falls could also occur while players pursued rewards. Token collection elicited decreased central theta (3.5-7 Hz) and frontal high alpha (10-12 Hz) power, alongside increased frontal beta (13-30 Hz) power. Goal reaching produced increases in parietal theta, frontal low alpha (8-10 Hz), widespread high alpha (frontal, central, parietal), and frontocentral beta power. Fall events showed decreased central theta and increased frontal and central beta power. Notably, all events were accompanied by increased beta-band activity, which the authors suggested might reflect motor engagement during eventrelated steering actions. Maclin et al. (2011) examined spectral responses to successful fortress hits in Space Fortress and to tones in an auditory oddball task performed alone or concurrently with gameplay. Fortress hits elicited delta (1.6-5 Hz) power increases around 500 ms at Fz and Pz. With training, deltaband ERSPs decreased for fortress hits and increased for oddball tones, paralleling the P3 changes described above. Training-related delta changes for fortress hits were negatively correlated with those for oddball tones. The authors interpreted this pattern as a reallocation of attentional resources as gameplay became more automated.

Eguinoa et al. (2026) used a custom-built endless-runner game resembling a probabilistic decision-making task. Participants navigated a penguin character on a forward-moving plane and selected between two doors under volatile, reciprocal reward contingencies across separate reward and punishment conditions. The authors introduced a novel EEG correlation framework, combining spatio-spectral decomposition with source power comodulation (SPoC), to identify datadriven spatial filters whose theta-(200-800 ms post-outcome) and alpha-band (1000-1800 ms post-outcome) power correlated with two target variables: trial-wise, model-derived precisionweighted prediction errors and individual state-anxiety scores. Theta power was positively, and alpha power negatively, correlated with prediction-error magnitude in both conditions, while theta power was negatively, and alpha power positively, correlated with state anxiety across participants. The resulting scalp and source-localized topographies differed between reward and punishment conditions in both analyses. Mathewson et al. (2012) reanalyzed the Space Fortress data of Maclin et al. (2011) and demonstrated that the same ERSPs associated with fortress hits were predictive not only of interindividual variability in Space Fortress gameplay performance but also of subsequent improvements in traditional cognitive tasks that rely on comparable cognitive processes. Specifically, pre-training delta-and alpha-band ERSPs following fortress hits were associated with enhanced performance in subsequent Space Fortress gameplay, as well as with superior outcomes on supplementary cognitive assessments, including the Stop task, the Sternberg memory test, and the N-Back task. Fortress-hit P3 amplitude correlated with initial game score but did not predict learning rate. McMahan et al. (2015) investigated whether a consumer-grade EEG system was capable of discriminating oscillatory neural activity specifically associated with death events from that elicited during general gameplay in Super Meat Boy. In this commercial platform game, players control a small cube-shaped character to traverse levels by jumping, running, and avoiding saw blades. The authors quantified spectral power in the beta (13-25 Hz) and gamma (25-43 Hz) frequency bands, computed as global averages across 14 electrodes within epochs extending from 100 ms prior to 750 ms following event onset. Notably, death events were associated with modulations in beta-and gamma-band power compared to general gameplay oscillatory activity, with gamma power higher during death events. Salminen and Ravaja (2008) examined event-locked oscillatory responses during gameplay of James Bond 007: NightFire, a first-person shooter (FPS) in which participants control a secret agent navigating a three-dimensional virtual environment and engaging opponents via strategic combat. The authors examined spectral changes around two events: wounding opponent characters and killing opponent characters. These events were identified by a frame-by-frame visual analysis of the recorded gameplay footage (25 frames per second, corresponding to a temporal resolution of 40 ms). Both events evoked increased occipital theta (4-6 Hz) activity relative to the pre-event baseline, with wounding events additionally eliciting increased occipital high theta (6-8 Hz) responses. Killing events elicited a low-alpha (8-10 Hz) asymmetry between central electrodes C3 and C4. Participants used the right index finger for shooting. Although the EEG signal was time-locked to the onset of visual feedback rather than to the motor response itself, the feedback events were elicited as direct consequences of the shooting action and were therefore likely to occur in close temporal proximity to the button press. This temporal contiguity renders the observed lateralized activity over motor-related cortical areas particularly noteworthy. However, no significant movement-related spectral patterns such as mu (10-12 Hz) or beta (16-20 Hz) ERD (Pfurtscheller and Aranibar, 1979; Pfurtscheller and Berghold, 1989) or post-movement beta ERS (Pfurtscheller et al., 1996; Pfurtscheller and Lopes da Silva, 2017) were observed. Shin et al. (2012) examined frontal alpha (8-12 Hz) asymmetry before and after outcome events in the commercial first-person combat game Counter-Strike 1.6. The authors compared hit events, defined as instances in which the participant successfully struck an opponent, with being-shot events, defined as instances in which the participant was struck by an opponent. Being-shot events were accompanied by a red light flashing on the screen. Notably, event onsets were annotated by a researcher who indicated their occurrence through the manual occlusion of a light-gate sensor. Frontal electrode asymmetry was quantified for the pre-event (-3 to 0 seconds) and post-event (7 to 10-seconds) intervals. Hit events were characterized by a transition from relatively greater left-hemispheric to relatively greater right-hemispheric alpha power, whereas being-shot events exhibited the inverse pattern. Rebert et al. (1984) employed the classic TV tennis game Pong to investigate interhemispheric alpha (8-13 Hz) asymmetry during a complex visuomotor task. Although the authors did not explicitly label individual game events, their analysis targeted the 10-second interval leading up to player misses that ended a rally. Within this pre-miss interval, the study documented alterations in alpha-band power asymmetry. Specifically, the authors reported a progressively increasing hemispheric asymmetry, characterized by reduced alpha power in the right relative to the left hemisphere at parietal and temporal electrode sites, whereas central electrode locations exhibited

S1. Canonical event-related EEG components

This section provides a brief overview of ERPs and event-related EEG time-frequency modulations commonly observed in laboratory paradigms, with a selective focus on those relevant to the present review. Consistent with the organization of the Results section of the main text, the components are categorized according to whether they are typically time-locked to stimulusrelated, response-related, or feedback-related events. These categories describe conventional time-locking rather than exclusive component-event relationships. A final subsection considers error and feedback processing in the motor domain. Latency, polarity, and scalp distribution provide descriptive characteristics, while functional interpretation also depends on the task, experimental contrasts, and observed sensitivities.

Stimulus-locked EEG components

P3 The P3 is a broad, positive deflection occurring approximately 250-600 ms post-stimulus, observed across a wide range of laboratory paradigms, including oddball tasks, visual search arrays, flanker tasks, and cued attention paradigms (Polich, 2007). Two subcomponents are typically distinguished: the earlier P3a (approximately 250-280 ms, frontocentral maximum), elicited by novel or unexpected stimuli regardless of task relevance, and thought to reflect stimulus-driven attentional orienting, and the later P3b (approximately 300-500 ms, centroparietal maximum), evoked by task-relevant stimuli (Ranganath and Rainer, 2003; Polich, 2007 Polich, , 2012)). In oddball paradigms, in which infrequent targets are embedded among frequent standards, P3b amplitude varies inversely with target probability. The cognitive process underlying P3b remains debated, with proposed accounts including cognitive processing, memory storage, context updating, decision processes, and stimulus-response link reactivation (Verleger, 2020). In dual-task designs, P3 amplitude is associated with attention allocation: when participants divide attention between concurrent tasks, the amplitude for a given stimulus decreases relative to single-task conditions, reflecting reduced attentional resources available for its processing (e.g., Isreal et al., 1980; Kramer et al., 1985; Luck and Kappenman, 2011; Polich, 2007). Reducing primary-task demands may therefore leave more resources available for a concurrent task, motivating a prediction of reciprocal P3 changes rather than guaranteeing that pattern.

N2

The N2 is a negative deflection peaking approximately 200-350 ms post-stimulus, with frontocentral and posterior scalp distributions depending on the paradigm and modality. Two broad classes of anterior N2 have been distinguished (Folstein and Van Petten, 2008). One class is associated with cognitive control and is elicited in trials involving competing or withheld motor responses, as in go/no-go and flanker tasks (Donkers and van Boxtel, 2004; Nieuwenhuis et al., 2003). The other class is associated with mismatch detection and is elicited when a stimulus deviates from an established perceptual template, such as a novel or deviant stimulus embedded among a sequence of repeated standards (Folstein and Van Petten, 2008). In both contexts, the N2 frequently co-occurs with the P3, particularly in oddball paradigms (Folstein and Van Petten, 2008; Polich, 2007).

or consequence. Reporting overall event frequencies is useful, but insufficient when the likelihood of an outcome changes with preceding actions, game state, or practice. Researchers should therefore document how probabilities and outcome magnitudes arise from the game mechanics and report their realized distributions across players and conditions. Where relevant, single-trial analyses can account for preceding events and changes in performance, although such analyses require sufficiently detailed gameplay records. Experimental control is also possible through the mechanics themselves.Yokota and Naruse (2021), for example, varied outcome probability through cannonball size and curvature, and modeled outcome magnitude through time pressure and damage level. Such designs make the variables of interest explicit, while requiring consideration of accompanying changes in sensory input or task demands. Distinguishing experimentally manipulated quantities from those that emerge during play is essential for relating game-elicited responses to findings from traditional laboratory tasks.RecommendationsThese challenges suggest that event-related analysis should inform the choice and design of the game from the outset. Custom-built or modifiable open-source games are particularly useful when the research question requires control over stimulus properties, event probabilities, or timing. When commercial titles are necessary, annotation procedures and their temporal uncertainty should be quantified and reported, following examples such asLi et al. (2025). Automated annotation methods developed in adjacent fields, including event-related fMRI, may offer additional tools for this purpose(Harel et al., 2026). In either case, logging should include neighboring actions and outcomes as well as the event of interest, so that their temporal relationships can inform analysis and interpretation. Physical stimulus properties and player perspective should be matched across the contrasts of interest where possible, while event probabilities and outcome magnitudes should be experimentally controlled or their realized distributions documented. Separating rewarding and punishing events in space and time can reduce ambiguity about which outcome elicited a response. Event spacing should accommodate the components under in-
